\[
D=(4,0),\quad E=(20,0),\quad F=(13c,13s),\quad G=(65c,65s),
\]

\[
[DEGF]=624s=288,
\]

\[
s=\frac{6}{13}.
\]

M=2F-D=(26c-4,26s),\qquad N=2E-G=(40-65c,-65s).

\[
[AFM]=26s=12,
\]
\[
[NEMF]=624s=288,
\]

[NECB]=624s=288.
\]

\[

Let \(A=(0,0)\), \(B=(6,0)\). Since \(\omega_1\) is internally tangent to \(\omega_2\), the center of \(\omega_2\) is \((-9,0)\), so \(C=(-24,0)\). Also \(D=(0,12)\).

Let the rectangle have half-width \(a\) and half-height \(b\), with vertices
\[
E=(a,-b),\quad F=(a,b),\quad G=(-a,b),\quad H=(-a,-b),
\]
and since it is inscribed in \(\omega_1\),
\[
a^2+b^2=36.
\]

Triangle areas:
\[
[DGF]=a(12-b),\qquad [CHG]=b(24-a).
\]
They are equal, so
\[
a(12-b)=b(24-a)\implies 12a=24b\implies a=2b.
\]

\[
(2b)^2+b^2=36\implies 5b^2=36\implies b^2=\frac{36}{5}.
\]

\[
4ab=4(2b)b=8b^2=8\cdot \frac{36}{5}=\frac{288}{5}.
\]
Thus \(m+n=288+5=293\).

Let a point \((x,y)\) on the original parabola also lie on the rotated image. Rotating back by \(60^\circ\) clockwise gives

\[
u=\frac{x+\sqrt3 y}{2},\qquad v=\frac{-\sqrt3 x+y}{2}.
\]

Since \((u,v)\) is on \(y=x^2-4\),

\[
\frac{-\sqrt3 x+y}{2}=\left(\frac{x+\sqrt3 y}{2}\right)^2-4.
\]

With \(y=x^2-4\), this becomes

\[
3x^4+2\sqrt3 x^3-25x^2-6\sqrt3 x+40=0.
\]

Factoring,

\[
(x^2+\sqrt3 x-4)(3x^2-\sqrt3 x-10)=0.
\]

The fourth-quadrant point comes from

\[
x^2+\sqrt3 x-4=0,
\]

so

\[
x=\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2}.
\]

Then

\[
y=x^2-4=\frac{11-\sqrt{57}}{2}-4=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}.
\]

Thus \(a=3\), \(b=57\), \(c=2\), and

\[
a+b+c=3+57+2=62.
\]

Answer: 62

For a regular 24-gon, chord length is determined by the cyclic distance \(d\in\{1,\dots,12\}\).  
For each \(d<12\), the graph connecting vertices at distance \(d\) decomposes into \(\gcd(24,d)\) cycles of length \(24/\gcd(24,d)\). A perfect matching exists only when each cycle has even length, and then each even cycle contributes 2 matchings.

\[

d=1:&\ 2^1=2\\
d=2:&\ 2^2=4\\

d=9:&\ 2^3=8\\
d=10:&\ 2^2=4\\
d=11:&\ 2^1=2
\end{aligned}
\]
Sum for \(d=1,\dots,11\) is \(112\).  
For \(d=12\), the segments are diameters, giving exactly 1 matching.

Total:
\[
112+1=113
\]

Let \(x_i = A_1A_i\). For each \(2\le i\le 10\),

\[
\frac12 x_i x_{i+1}\sin\theta=1,\qquad \cos\theta=\frac{12}{13},\quad \sin\theta=\frac5{13}.
\]

So

\[
x_i x_{i+1}=\frac{26}{5}.
\]

Thus the lengths from \(A_1\) alternate: \(x_2=x_4=\cdots=x_{10}=a\), and \(x_3=x_5=\cdots=x_{11}=b\), with

\[
ab=\frac{26}{5}.
\]

Let \(t=a+b=A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}\). Each side \(A_iA_{i+1}\) for \(2\le i\le 10\) has length

\[
s^2=a^2+b^2-2ab\cos\theta
=t^2-2ab-\frac{24}{13}ab
=t^2-\frac{52}{5}-\frac{48}{5}
=t^2-20.
\]

The perimeter is

\[
t+9\sqrt{t^2-20}=20.
\]

So

\[
9\sqrt{t^2-20}=20-t.
\]

Squaring:

\[
81(t^2-20)=(20-t)^2
\]

\[
80t^2+40t-2020=0
\]

\[
4t^2+2t-101=0.
\]

Thus

\[
t=\frac{-1+9\sqrt5}{4}.
\]

So \(m=9,n=5,p=1,q=4\), and

\[
m+n+p+q=9+5+1+4=19.
\]

Answer: 19

So P_B(different arcs) = (1/6) sum over unordered pairs {r,s} of measure of (u∈I1∩Q_r, v∈I2∩Q_s or vice versa) / (area Q_r * area Q_s). Each quadrant length π/2, so product area = (π/2)^2. For each ordered pair r≠s, probability = (2/(π/2)^2?) Let's formulate.

For unordered pair {r,s}, the conditional probability given A is:
P_{rs}(A) = [ m(I1∩Q_r)*m(I2∩Q_s)+ m(I1∩Q_s)*m(I2∩Q_r) ] / ((π/2)^2), where m is arc length measure. Because u in Q_r, v in Q_s independent uniform; need one in I1 other in I2.

So P_B(A) = (1/12) * (4/π^2?) Let's set quadrant length L=π/2. m(I∩Q)/L are normalized lengths in [0,1]. For ordered r≠s:

Then P_B(A)= (1/12) Σ_{r≠s} (m1_r/L)(m2_s/L).

But note Σ_{r} m1_r = d (length of I1), Σ_s m2_s = 2π-d. Also Σ_{r≠s} m1_r m2_s = (Σ_r m1_r)(Σ_s m2_s) - Σ_r m1_r m2_r. So
P_B(A)= (1/12 L^2)[ d(2π-d) - Σ_r m1_r m2_r ].

Where m1_r,m2_r are lengths of I1 and I2 in quadrant r. Since I1 and I2 partition circle, for each quadrant r, m1_r + m2_r = L (because quadrant length L). So Σ_r m1_r m2_r = Σ_r m1_r (L - m1_r) = L Σ_r m1_r - Σ_r m1_r^2 = L d - Σ m1_r^2. Thus
P_B(A)= (1/(12 L^2))[ d(2π-d) - L d + Σ m1_r^2 ] = (1/(12 L^2))[ d(2π - d - L) + Σ m1_r^2 ].

We need to analyze the C function for vulnerabilities. The function reads a length field from wire (body_len), computes pad, encoded_len = body_len + pad. Checks if encoded_len > wire_left - 4. If not, copies encoded_len bytes into scratch of size AUTH_STACK_MAX (256). Then calls gss_verify_mic(scratch, body_len).

Potential vulnerability: buffer overflow in memcpy if encoded_len > AUTH_STACK_MAX. The check only ensures encoded_len <= wire_left - 4, but doesn't ensure it fits in scratch. Since body_len is attacker-controlled, can be large up to wire_left-4 (which could be huge). But scratch is fixed 256 bytes. So memcpy can overflow stack if encoded_len > 256. The check at line 17 only checks against wire_left, not scratch size. So primary bug: missing bounds check against AUTH_STACK_MAX before memcpy. Which line introduces the bug? The vulnerability is that the code copies encoded_len bytes into a fixed-size buffer without checking it fits. The bug is introduced at line 20 (memcpy) or maybe earlier at line 10 (declaration of scratch) but the primary issue is lack of check. Typically, the line where the overflow occurs is line 20. But we need to identify the line where the bug is introduced. Could be line 17: the check only ensures source bytes exist, not destination capacity. The comment says "This check only proves the source bytes exist in the packet." So the bug is that the check doesn't include scratch size. The primary bug is at line 20 (memcpy) because it copies without bounds. But often in such questions, they ask for the line where the vulnerability is introduced. Since the code is a function, the bug is the missing check before memcpy. The line number of memcpy is 20. However, we might consider that the bug is at line 17 because the condition should also include encoded_len <= AUTH_STACK_MAX. But the actual overflow occurs at line 20. The question: "Reply with the single best line number where the primary bug is introduced." Usually, it's the line where the unsafe operation happens (memcpy). So answer: 20.

But let's double-check: Could there be integer overflow? body_len is uint32_t, pad computed from body_len & 3. encoded_len = body_len + pad. Since body_len can be up to 0xFFFFFFFF, adding pad could wrap? pad is at most 3, so if body_len is near max, encoded_len could wrap to small value, bypassing check. But wire_left is size_t (likely 64-bit). The check: encoded_len > wire_left - 4. If encoded_len wraps, it might be small and pass, but then memcpy copies encoded_len bytes which are small? Actually if body_len = 0xFFFFFFFF, pad = (4 - (3)) & 3 = 1, encoded_len = 0x100000000 which wraps to 0 on 32-bit? But uint32_t addition: body_len + pad is computed in uint32_t arithmetic, so it wraps modulo 2^32. So encoded_len could be small (e.g., 0). Then check passes if wire_left -4 >= 0 (always true for size_t). Then memcpy copies 0 bytes, no overflow. But then gss_verify_mic(scratch, body_len) with body_len huge? That might cause issues but not buffer overflow. However, the primary vulnerability is stack overflow due to large encoded_len > 256. So line 20.

But also note: scratch is on stack, size 256. The check at line 17 only ensures encoded_len <= wire_left -4, which could be much larger than 256. So memcpy will overflow. So answer: 20.

Let's consider if there are other issues: pad calculation: (4 - (body_len & 3)) & 3. If body_len is multiple of 4, pad=0. Otherwise pad = 4 - remainder. That's correct for XDR padding. But encoded_len could be up to wire_left-4, but if wire_left is large, overflow.

Thus answer: 20.

We need to analyze the C function for vulnerabilities. The function parses an IPv6 address from a string into a 16-byte array. It handles "::" compression.

Let's examine the code carefully.

The function:

```c
static int parse_ipv6_reduced(const char *src, uint8_t dst[16])
{
    uint8_t tmp[16];
    uint8_t *tp = tmp;
    uint8_t *colonp = NULL;

    if (src[0] == ':' && src[1] == ':') {
        colonp = --tp;          // remember compression point at the beginning
        src += 2;
    }

    while (*src != '\0') {
        unsigned v = parse_hextet(&src);
        *tp++ = (uint8_t)(v >> 8);
        *tp++ = (uint8_t)v;
        if (*src == ':') src++;
    }

    if (colonp != NULL) {
        size_t n = (size_t)(tp - colonp);
        memmove(colonp + (16 - (tp - tmp)), colonp, n);
        memset(colonp, 0, 16 - (tp - tmp));
    }

    memcpy(dst, tmp, 16);
    return 0;
}
```

We need to identify if there is a vulnerability. The function seems to parse IPv6 address with possible "::" compression. It uses `tmp` buffer of size 16. `tp` starts at `tmp`. If the input starts with "::", it sets `colonp = --tp;` which decrements `tp` before assigning. That means `colonp` points to one byte before `tmp`, i.e., `tmp[-1]`. This is out-of-bounds. Then later, when shifting, it uses `colonp` as a base for memmove and memset. This could cause buffer overflow or underflow.

Let's analyze the logic:

- `tp` initially points to `tmp[0]`.
- If input starts with "::", `colonp = --tp;` makes `tp` point to `tmp[-1]`, and `colonp` also points to `tmp[-1]`. Then `src += 2` skips the two colons.
- Then parsing continues: each hextet writes two bytes at `*tp++` (so first write goes to `tmp[-1]`? Actually after decrement, `tp` points to `tmp[-1]`, so writing `*tp++ = ...` writes to `tmp[-1]` and then increments to `tmp[0]`. That's an out-of-bounds write before the array. This is a buffer underflow.

But wait: The typical pattern for IPv6 parsing with "::" is to remember the position where compression occurred, but they usually set `colonp = tp` (not decrement). Here they do `--tp`, which is suspicious. Let's see the intended logic: If input starts with "::", then there are no hextets before the compression point. The compression point should be at the beginning of the array, i.e., `tmp[0]`. But they set `colonp = --tp` so it points to `tmp[-1]`. Then when parsing, the first hextet will write to `tmp[-1]` and `tmp[0]`? Actually `*tp++` writes at current `tp`, then increments. So first write goes to `tmp[-1]`, second write goes to `tmp[0]`. That's clearly wrong.

But maybe the code is intended to handle the case where there are no hextets before "::"? Let's think: If input starts with "::", then after skipping, we parse hextets. The compression point should be at the beginning of the array (index 0). But they set `colonp` to `tmp[-1]`. Then later, when shifting, they compute `n = tp - colonp`. Since `tp` will have advanced by number of bytes written. If we write two bytes per hextet, after parsing all hextets, `tp` will be somewhere. The shift logic: `memmove(colonp + (16 - (tp - tmp)), colonp, n);` and `memset(colonp, 0, 16 - (tp - tmp));`. This is intended to move the parsed bytes after the compression point to the end of the array, and zero-fill the gap. But because `colonp` is off by one, the calculations will be wrong.

Let's simulate a simple case: Input "::1" (IPv6 loopback). The code:

- src[0]=':', src[1]=':' => true.

- Loop ends.

- memmove(colonp + (16 - (tp - tmp)), colonp, n). tp - tmp = 1 (since tp points to tmp[1], tmp points to tmp[0]). So 16 - 1 = 15. colonp + 15 = tmp[-1] + 15 = tmp[14]. memmove from colonp (tmp[-1]) length 2 to tmp[14]. That copies tmp[-1] and tmp[0] to tmp[14] and tmp[15]. But tmp[-1] is out-of-bounds read. Also the destination is within array but source is out-of-bounds.

Actually it writes 15 bytes starting at tmp[-1], which covers tmp[-1] through tmp[13]. That's an underflow write.

So memmove copies from colonp (tmp[-1]) and tmp[0] to tmp[14] and tmp[15]. At that time, tmp[-1] is uninitialized (or whatever), tmp[0] is 1. typedef int (*ule_handler)(struct skb *, const uint8_t *, size_t);
static ule_handler mandatory_handlers[255];

static int handle_ule_ext(struct skb *skb, uint16_t type,
                          const uint8_t *p, size_t len)
{
    uint8_t htype = (uint8_t)(type & 0x00ff);

    if (htype < 0x80)
        return handle_optional_ext(skb, htype, p, len);

    if (mandatory_handlers[htype] != NULL)

The extension type is the low byte of a 16-bit protocol field. The mandatory-extension table has handlers for values 0 through 254.

We need to analyze the C function for a vulnerability. The function verifies a DFA with reduced states. Some states are encoded as differences from another state default table.

Let's understand the code:

```
static int verify_dfa_reduced(const struct dfa *d)
{
    for (uint32_t state = 0; state < d->state_count; state++) {
        uint32_t j = state;

        if (d->default_table[j] >= d->state_count)
            return -EINVAL;

        while (d->is_differential[j]) {
            uint32_t parent = d->default_table[j];
            j = parent;                    // parent not rechecked here
        }

        validate_accept_table(d->accept[j]);
    }
    return 0;
}
```
